People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… I guess I’m here because I haven’t been myself since my friend from choir passed away. It was so sudden, and I keep thinking I should be handling things better, but I’m just not.

Little things are setting me off now. Like, I’ll be looking over the potluck list for church, something I’ve done a hundred times, and I’ll just start crying. Or someone calls me about planning something, and instead of just answering and sorting it out like I normally would, I get all worked up and overwhelmed. And I’ve even started missing church planning meetings and things I never would have missed before. That’s not like me at all.

I don’t know… I just feel like since she died, everything that used to feel manageable feels heavy, and I’m embarrassed by how much it’s affecting me.

I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.

I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.

There are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.

I haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.

And I’ve even started missing church planning meetings and things I never would have missed before. That’s not like me at all.\n\nI don’t know… I just feel like since she died, everything that used to feel manageable feels heavy, and I’m embarrassed by how much it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. I keep thinking it should feel like longer or maybe shorter, I don’t know… time has felt strange since she died. But yes, about a month since my close friend from choir passed away, and that’s really when all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine presence of guilt", "question": "Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?"}

I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.

When you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Adjustment Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.

I mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.

With the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.

There are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.

I suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I haven’t been myself since my friend from choir passed away. It was so sudden, and I keep thinking I should be handling things better, but I’m just not.\n\nLittle things are setting me off now. Like, I’ll be looking over the potluck list for church, something I’ve done a hundred times, and I’ll just start crying. Or someone calls me about planning something, and instead of just answering and sorting it out like I normally would, I get all worked up and overwhelmed. And I’ve even started missing church planning meetings and things I never would have missed before. That’s not like me at all.\n\nI don’t know… I just feel like since she died, everything that used to feel manageable feels heavy, and I’m embarrassed by how much it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. I keep thinking it should feel like longer or maybe shorter, I don’t know… time has felt strange since she died. But yes, about a month since my close friend from choir passed away, and that’s really when all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.

It’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.

But I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.

Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.

But lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.

So, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.

Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.

Some things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.

But I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.

I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.\n\nSome things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. That sounds like I’ve just stopped caring about the things I used to enjoy, and I don’t think that’s quite right.

I still care about them, and I still sometimes want to be part of them. Like with church things, or being around people I know, I don’t feel completely detached from it. But it’s different than before. I may not feel the same pull to get up and do it, or I may have to remind myself that it usually feels better if I go or if I participate. It’s not as automatic.

So… maybe it’s not that the interest is gone. It’s more like it gets covered over, or it takes more effort to reach. I can still enjoy things at times, but I don’t seem to move toward them as naturally as I used to.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I haven’t been myself since my friend from choir passed away. It was so sudden, and I keep thinking I should be handling things better, but I’m just not.\n\nLittle things are setting me off now. Like, I’ll be looking over the potluck list for church, something I’ve done a hundred times, and I’ll just start crying. Or someone calls me about planning something, and instead of just answering and sorting it out like I normally would, I get all worked up and overwhelmed. And I’ve even started missing church planning meetings and things I never would have missed before. That’s not like me at all.\n\nI don’t know… I just feel like since she died, everything that used to feel manageable feels heavy, and I’m embarrassed by how much it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. I keep thinking it should feel like longer or maybe shorter, I don’t know… time has felt strange since she died. But yes, about a month since my close friend from choir passed away, and that’s really when all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.\n\nSome things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. That sounds like I’ve just stopped caring about the things I used to enjoy, and I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nI still care about them, and I still sometimes want to be part of them. Like with church things, or being around people I know, I don’t feel completely detached from it. But it’s different than before. I may not feel the same pull to get up and do it, or I may have to remind myself that it usually feels better if I go or if I participate. It’s not as automatic.\n\nSo… maybe it’s not that the interest is gone. It’s more like it gets covered over, or it takes more effort to reach. I can still enjoy things at times, but I don’t seem to move toward them as naturally as I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Adjustment Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.\n\nSome things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. That sounds like I’ve just stopped caring about the things I used to enjoy, and I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nI still care about them, and I still sometimes want to be part of them. Like with church things, or being around people I know, I don’t feel completely detached from it. But it’s different than before. I may not feel the same pull to get up and do it, or I may have to remind myself that it usually feels better if I go or if I participate. It’s not as automatic.\n\nSo… maybe it’s not that the interest is gone. It’s more like it gets covered over, or it takes more effort to reach. I can still enjoy things at times, but I don’t seem to move toward them as naturally as I used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have less interest in activities, such as singing in the choir, compared to before your friend passed away?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 63 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response\nmanifestation: \"Ever since my choir friend passed so suddenly, I just haven't been myself—I cry over the potluck lists, get all worked up about calls I used to handle easy, and I've started missing church planning things I never would have missed before.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, with choir especially it’s hard to separate it from her, because she was my choir friend, and her passing was so sudden. Ever since then I just haven’t felt like myself walking into those church things.

It isn’t that I don’t care about choir or church anymore. I do. But things that used to feel simple now feel… heavy. I’ll look at the potluck lists or the calls that need to be made, and I’ll just get all worked up over them, which is not how I used to be. Those were things I could handle easily before. I’d make the calls, keep track of who was bringing what, know which planning meeting I needed to be at — all of that was just part of what I did.

Now I’ve started missing church planning things I never would have missed before. And sometimes even thinking about choir or the people connected to it brings up all of that sadness again, so I suppose I don’t have the same interest or ease with it that I did before she died. It’s not gone completely, but it’s definitely changed.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.21, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I haven’t been myself since my friend from choir passed away. It was so sudden, and I keep thinking I should be handling things better, but I’m just not.\n\nLittle things are setting me off now. Like, I’ll be looking over the potluck list for church, something I’ve done a hundred times, and I’ll just start crying. Or someone calls me about planning something, and instead of just answering and sorting it out like I normally would, I get all worked up and overwhelmed. And I’ve even started missing church planning meetings and things I never would have missed before. That’s not like me at all.\n\nI don’t know… I just feel like since she died, everything that used to feel manageable feels heavy, and I’m embarrassed by how much it’s affecting me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month now. I keep thinking it should feel like longer or maybe shorter, I don’t know… time has felt strange since she died. But yes, about a month since my close friend from choir passed away, and that’s really when all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.\n\nSome things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. That sounds like I’ve just stopped caring about the things I used to enjoy, and I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nI still care about them, and I still sometimes want to be part of them. I may not feel the same pull to get up and do it, or I may have to remind myself that it usually feels better if I go or if I participate. I can still enjoy things at times, but I don’t seem to move toward them as naturally as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less interest in activities, such as singing in the choir, compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, compared to before she passed, I do have less interest, or maybe less ability to get myself into things. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring about everything, because I haven’t. But ever since my choir friend died so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.

It shows up most with church things, because that’s where so much of my routine and my people are. Before, I could handle the potluck lists, the phone calls, the planning meetings — all those little things that needed doing. I didn’t even think twice about them. Now I’ll look at a list or think about making calls, and I’ll get worked up, even cry over it sometimes, which feels so unlike me.

And I’ve started missing church planning things I never would have missed before. That part bothers me, because I was always someone people could count on. So yes, I guess my interest in most of those usual activities is less than it was before, but it feels tied to how much everything has changed since she died.

I keep thinking it should feel like longer or maybe shorter, I don’t know… time has felt strange since she died. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. But yes, about a month since my close friend from choir passed away, and that’s really when all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Are you still enjoying the things you used to like doing, like singing in the choir?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. Singing used to feel very natural to me, like something I didn’t even have to think about — just being there with everyone, hearing the harmonies, knowing where to stand, what music we were working on, all of that.\n\nLately it feels different, but I don’t know if I’d say I don’t enjoy it exactly. It’s more like I’m not myself while I’m doing it. Sometimes I’m there and I’m trying to sing, and I’m aware of who’s missing, and then it doesn’t feel the same. But I can’t quite tell if the enjoyment is gone or if everything is just tangled up with missing her right now. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I keep trying to think of something I could point to and say, “Yes, that still feels good the way it used to,” but it’s hard to separate what I actually enjoy from what I’m just doing out of habit.\n\nThere are moments, I suppose — like if I’m sitting quietly with a cup of tea, or if someone says something kind, I can feel a little bit of comfort. But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly. It’s more like… a brief settling, and then I’m aware again that things feel different.\n\nI haven’t really been paying close attention to it that way, to be honest. I think I’ve just been trying to get through the days and do what I’m supposed to do. So I don’t want to say there’s nothing at all, because that sounds too dramatic, but I also can’t think of much that feels genuinely enjoyable right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I feel guilty exactly… or at least I haven’t put that word to it. I keep turning things over in my mind, but I’m not sure whether that’s guilt or just missing her and wishing things had been different.\n\nWhen you say guilty, do you mean like feeling responsible for what happened? Because I don’t think I feel that. It’s more… I wonder if I should have said something different, or been more present, or appreciated things more when she was here. But I’m not sure that’s guilt so much as regret, maybe. It’s hard to separate all those feelings right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend passed away because they no longer bring you any pleasure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s anything I’ve completely stopped doing for that reason, no. At least, not that I can clearly identify.\n\nI mean, there are things I do less, or things I approach differently, but it’s hard for me to say, “I stopped because there’s no pleasure in it anymore.” Sometimes I avoid something because it reminds me of her, or because I feel tired of explaining myself, or because I’m not sure how I’ll feel once I’m there. But that’s not exactly the same as deciding it brings me no pleasure.\n\nWith the choir, for example, I wouldn’t say I’ve fully stopped because I get nothing from it. It’s more complicated than that. Some parts still feel familiar, and then other parts feel painful or strange. So I suppose my answer is: I haven’t noticed any activity that I’ve entirely given up just because it stopped being enjoyable. It’s more that everything feels a bit mixed up right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest exactly. That feels like such a definite way to put it, and I don’t know if that’s quite true.\n\nThere are things I used to care about that feel… different now. Like I might still know, in my head, that they matter to me, but I don’t always feel pulled toward them in the same way. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to enjoy that,” and then I just don’t quite have the same feeling behind it. But I don’t know whether that’s losing interest or just everything being colored by what happened.\n\nI suppose I still care about the things I cared about before, at least in some form. It’s more that my attention doesn’t settle on them as easily. They don’t feel as simple or uncomplicated. So… maybe a little, but I can’t honestly say I’ve stopped caring. It’s more like the caring is there, but it’s quieter, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer look forward to things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I no longer look forward to things. That sounds very absolute, and I’m not sure that’s true for me.\n\nIt’s more like… the feeling of looking forward to something is less clear than it used to be. Before, if there was something coming up, I might feel a little lift from it, or I’d think about it ahead of time. Now I may still make plans, or know that something should be nice, but the anticipation doesn’t always come as easily. Sometimes I don’t really know how I’m going to feel until I’m actually there.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say there’s nothing I look forward to anymore. It’s just changed. Things feel more uncertain, maybe less simple. I can still imagine enjoying something, but it’s mixed in with other feelings now, so it doesn’t feel as straightforward as it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're putting less effort or energy into the things you're still doing compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that is happening. I don’t always like admitting it, because I’ve always been someone people could count on, especially at church. I was used to keeping track of things — making calls, organizing lists, helping with the luncheon details, all of that. Not in a dramatic way, just the ordinary things that need doing and that I usually would just handle.\n\nBut lately I’m not keeping up the way I always did before. I’ll mean to call someone, or update a list, or check on part of the luncheon arrangements, and then it either takes me much longer or I realize someone else has already stepped in because I haven’t gotten to it. And I have had to let other church members take over some of those calls and lists and parts of the luncheon, which feels… uncomfortable. Not because they aren’t capable — they are — but because that used to be my role, and I didn’t need so much help with it.\n\nSo, yes, I’m still doing some things, but I don’t think I’m putting the same energy into them. Or maybe I try to, but it doesn’t come out the same. It feels like I’m moving slower, or I can’t hold all the pieces in my head the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things that used to bring you pleasure now feel empty or unfulfilling?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I would say empty, exactly. That word feels a little too… final, maybe. Like I sit there and feel absolutely nothing, and I don’t know that that’s always true.\n\nSome things do feel less satisfying than they used to, I suppose. If I’m with people from church, or doing something that normally would have felt warm or meaningful, I can still recognize that it’s good to be there. I can still appreciate parts of it. But it doesn’t always land in the same place inside me, if that makes sense. It’s like I know something should be comforting or pleasant, but the feeling is a little muted.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say everything feels empty. That doesn’t feel fair to say. It’s more that pleasure feels harder to hold onto, or it doesn’t come as easily or as fully as it used to. Sometimes I only notice afterward that something was nice, rather than feeling it clearly in the moment. So… unfulfilling, maybe sometimes, but not completely empty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to find enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a full loss of interest. That sounds like I’ve just stopped caring about the things I used to enjoy, and I don’t think that’s quite right.\n\nI still care about them, and I still sometimes want to be part of them. Like with church things, or being around people I know, I don’t feel completely detached from it. But it’s different than before. I may not feel the same pull to get up and do it, or I may have to remind myself that it usually feels better if I go or if I participate. It’s not as automatic.\n\nSo… maybe it’s not that the interest is gone. It’s more like it gets covered over, or it takes more effort to reach. I can still enjoy things at times, but I don’t seem to move toward them as naturally as I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less interest in activities, such as singing in the choir, compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, with choir especially it’s hard to separate it from her, because she was my choir friend, and her passing was so sudden. Ever since then I just haven’t felt like myself walking into those church things.\n\nIt isn’t that I don’t care about choir or church anymore. I do. But things that used to feel simple now feel… heavy. I’ll look at the potluck lists or the calls that need to be made, and I’ll just get all worked up over them, which is not how I used to be. Those were things I could handle easily before. I’d make the calls, keep track of who was bringing what, know which planning meeting I needed to be at — all of that was just part of what I did.\n\nNow I’ve started missing church planning things I never would have missed before. And sometimes even thinking about choir or the people connected to it brings up all of that sadness again, so I suppose I don’t have the same interest or ease with it that I did before she died. It’s not gone completely, but it’s definitely changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have less interest in most activities compared to before your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, compared to before she passed, I do have less interest, or maybe less ability to get myself into things. I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve stopped caring about everything, because I haven’t. But ever since my choir friend died so suddenly, I just haven’t been myself.\n\nIt shows up most with church things, because that’s where so much of my routine and my people are. Before, I could handle the potluck lists, the phone calls, the planning meetings — all those little things that needed doing. I didn’t even think twice about them. Now I’ll look at a list or think about making calls, and I’ll get worked up, even cry over it sometimes, which feels so unlike me.\n\nAnd I’ve started missing church planning things I never would have missed before. That part bothers me, because I was always someone people could count on. So yes, I guess my interest in most of those usual activities is less than it was before, but it feels tied to how much everything has changed since she died.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Some things still can, yes… but it’s not the same kind of easy joy I used to have. Before my choir friend passed so suddenly, I could just go into those church activities and feel useful, connected, part of everything. Choir, planning things, even the potluck lists and calls — I know that sounds small, but those were things I handled without thinking. They gave me a sense of purpose, I guess.

Now, even when I want to enjoy them, I’ll get tangled up in it. I’ll look at a potluck list and start crying, or I’ll think about making calls I used to make easily and get all worked up. And I’ve started missing church planning things I never would have missed before. That doesn’t feel like me at all.

So there may still be moments where I feel glad to be around people or feel a little bit of that old feeling, but it’s harder to get there now. It’s like the joy is still somewhere underneath, but all of this sadness and stress since she died gets in the way.

But I don’t know if I’d call that pleasure exactly.
